<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Which residue is the proton acceptor at the active site?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Aspartate at position 232<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Aspartate at position 244<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Glutamate at position 235<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Aspartate at position 250<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: <br><br>**Step 1: Identify the enzyme type**<br>Analyzing the sequence for characteristic motifs, I identify two key aspartic protease signatures:<br>- **&quot;D-I-T-G&quot; motif** at positions 244-247 (D244, I245, T246, G247) — a variant of the conserved &quot;D-x-T-G&quot; catalytic motif found in aspartic proteases<br>- **&quot;D-A-T&quot; motif** at positions 403-405 (D403, A404, T405) — the complementary catalytic motif<br><br>The protein is ~419 residues (~47 kDa), consistent with cathepsin-like aspartic proteases.<br><br>**Step 2: Understand the catalytic mechanism**<br>In aspartic proteases, two catalytic Asp residues participate in the hydrolysis of peptide bonds:<br>- One Asp acts as a **general base** (proton acceptor) — it deprotonates a bridging water molecule, generating a hydroxide ion that nucleophilically attacks the scissile peptide bond<br>- The other Asp acts as a **general acid** (proton donor) — it protonates the leaving amine group<br><br>**Step 3: Determine which residue is the proton acceptor**<br>In cathepsin D-type aspartic proteases, the Asp in the **D-x-T-G motif** serves as the **general base (proton acceptor)**, while the Asp in the **D-A-T motif** serves as the general acid. This is consistent with studies showing that in cathepsin D, the Asp in the DIT motif (equivalent to D244 here) is the one that accepts a proton from the catalytic water molecule.<br><br>**Step 4: Evaluate options**<br>- A (D232): Not part of a catalytic motif — incorrect<br>- B (D244): Part of the D-I-T-G motif — this is the catalytic Asp acting as the proton acceptor ✓<br>- C (E235): Not part of a catalytic motif — incorrect<br>- D (D250): Not in a catalytic motif — incorrect<br><br>2. answer: B</td>

The catalytic mechanism involves:

4. The other Asp (protonated) acts as a general acid → it donates a proton to the leaving amine nitrogen

So the &quot;proton acceptor&quot; is the general base Asp that abstracts the proton from water.

<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> What biological function is best supported for this protein?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> A branched-chain amino-acid aminotransferase that primarily acts on valine, leucine, and isoleucine.<br><br><strong>B.</strong> An aromatic-amino-acid aminotransferase that transfers amino groups from phenylalanine, tyrosine, and tryptophan to 2-oxoglutarate, producing aromatic keto acids and glutamate.<br><br><strong>C.</strong> An aspartate aminotransferase that transfers an amino group from aspartate to 2-oxoglutarate, producing oxaloacetate and glutamate.<br><br><strong>D.</strong> A glutamate dehydrogenase that interconverts glutamate and 2-oxoglutarate while releasing or assimilating ammonia.<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: <br><br>**Sequence Analysis:**<br>- The protein is approximately 390 amino acids long, consistent with Class III fold-type I aminotransferases (typically 400-470 aa).<br>- The N-terminal region contains &quot;VISLAGG**LPAPETFP**&quot; — the &quot;GG&quot; motif in the first ββα-motif is characteristic of fold-type I aminotransferases.<br>- The middle region shows &quot;**KGFTPLRLALAEW**&quot; — the &quot;FT&quot; (equivalent to &quot;FG&quot; in canonical AATs) near position ~85 and the &quot;RL&quot; pair are hallmarks of aromatic amino acid aminotransferases (AATs).<br>- The catalytic lysine region &quot;**GEPVKPIK**&quot; matches the PLP-binding lysine context found in AATs.<br>- The motif &quot;**VYTIPTFQNP**&quot; contains a conserved phenylalanine and asparagine critical for binding aromatic substrates in AATs.<br><br>**Eliminating other options:**<br>- **Option A (BCAT):** Branched-chain amino acid aminotransferases use fold-type IV (distinct structure, dimer of dimers) with completely different sequence motifs. - His: acts as a proton acceptor (general base)

The Claisen condensation reaction in thiolases:
1. The substrate (acetyl-CoA) binds
2. The active site Cys attacks the thioester carbonyl, forming an acyl-enzyme intermediate (Cys-S-CO-R)
3. The His deprotonates the α-carbon of the substrate (or the acyl-enzyme intermediate) to form an enolate
4. The enolate attacks the carbonyl of the second substrate molecule
5. The product is released

So in the standard Type II thiolase mechanism:
- Cys forms the acyl-enzyme thioester intermediate ✓
- His acts as a general base (abstracts/accepts a proton from the α-carbon)
